## Part 6: Qualitative Biographical Case Studies of Grassroots Fellow Leaders

### 6.1 Manju: Educational Leadership, Floodland Resilience, and Gender Vanguard
- **Geographic & Social Identity**: Resident of Mihinpurwa Block, Bahraich District (associated with Bhattha Bargadaha village in community records); smallholder farmer managing 3 Bighas of household land; active member of the Aajeevika Adhikar Sangathan (AAS).
- **Early Life of Deprivation and Abuse**:
  Manju’s early life was marked by acute loss. Following her father's death during her childhood, her mother worked tirelessly as a daily wage laborer to sustain the household and keep the children in school. Before Manju could complete her basic schooling, she was married off at a young age. Entering married life without awareness of her legal rights or personal agency, she endured years of emotional, physical, and social hardship. Her mobility was strictly restricted, decisions were made for her, and her daily existence was characterized by humiliation and fear. As she reflected: *"I thought this was my fate. I did not know that my life could be different."*
- **The Awakening: Peeping Through the Window to Grassroots Leadership**:
  A critical turning point occurred when women's meetings supported through the JAI AZADI / AAS program began in her village. Initially afraid to attend, Manju listened from her windows, observing facilitators who spoke about rights, equality, self-respect, and collective solidarity. Gathering courage, she stepped forward and joined the group. Finding a space where women shared experiences and supported one another, she came to understand that violence was not destiny and silence was not strength: *"When I heard other women speak, I realized I was not alone. That day something changed inside me."*
- **The Community Campaign for a Village School**:
  In her locality, children faced long, difficult walks along unsafe roads to reach the nearest primary school, leading many children to drop out. Determined that children should have access to education, Manju initiated an advocacy campaign that spanned nearly three years.
  Through interface meetings with officials at the village, block, and district levels, she made approximately 8 to 10 formal visits to administrative authorities. When progress stalled, she utilized public grievance systems, including the Chief Minister's *Jan Sunvayi* Helpline (1076), and raised the demand in village assemblies. Despite discouragement from skeptics who doubted that a rural woman could influence administrative decisions, Manju persisted: *"There were nights I cried, but the next morning I stood up again. I knew this fight was not only for my children, but for every child."* Her persistent advocacy bore fruit when administrative processes for village school construction were formally approved and commenced.
- **Agronomic Adaptation in the *Khala* Lowlands**:
  Manju’s household held 3 Bighas of land, of which 2 Bighas were situated in the *Khala* (a depressed, low-lying area) prone to severe waterlogging where standing crops frequently drowned. Drawing courage from her collective engagement, Manju and her family assessed the risk and decided to plant sugarcane across all three Bighas, intercropping seasonal vegetables using seeds and organic compost methods supported by DEHAT. When monsoon inundation arrived, the water-tolerant sugarcane survived the waterlogging. While noting that *"the income may be less, but the experience gained is very valuable"* (Grant Report 2025, p. 34), the low-lying plots ceased to be an economic liability and became a dependable source of household sustenance.
- **Current Vanguard Role**:
  Today, Manju is recognized as a dedicated grassroots leader. She travels outside her locality—visiting Bahraich, Lucknow, and Rajasthan (participating in the VAAGDHARA agro-ecology exposure visit in January 2026). She actively supports women experiencing domestic violence, speaks confidently in public meetings, and exemplifies how individual resilience and collective organization foster lasting community leadership.

### 6.2 Shanti Devi: Transforming Child Marriage Trauma into Collective Agency and Functional Literacy
- **Geographic & Social Identity**: Resident of Bajpur Bankati village, Mihinpurwa Block (documented in case records and Grant Report 2025); smallholder farmer, community facilitator, and mother.
- **Early Hardship, Physical Abuse, and Abandonment**:
  Shanti’s father passed away during her childhood, leaving her mother to support the household through grueling daily wage labor while striving to educate her children. Facing severe economic deprivation, Shanti was married off as a child. In her marital home, she was subjected to continuous physical abuse and emotional trauma over trivial household matters. Because her menstrual cycles were delayed and irregular, her marital family and husband subjected her to degrading verbal abuse. Eventually, her husband abandoned her entirely, leaving her destitute. She returned to her mother’s home, where she was treated by extended relatives and village society as an economic burden.
- **Maternal Tragedy and Compassionate Adoption**:
  After two years of separation, her husband returned, and Shanti subsequently gave birth to two children. Tragically, both young children passed away during early childhood. Shattered by profound grief, Shanti struggled to rebuild her emotional life.
  During this period of hardship, a woman in a neighboring village passed away during childbirth, leaving behind a newborn infant girl without parental care. In a profound act of maternal empathy, Shanti stepped forward and adopted the infant girl as her own daughter. Defying conservative village criticism, Shanti poured her life into caring for the child: *"This decision came from the heart, not out of any compulsion. With her arrival, my lonely life found its glow again. I became a mother once more"* (Grant Report 2025, p. 30).
- **Collective Solidarity and Literacy Transformation**:
  When DEHAT organized women's meetings in her locality, Shanti attended seeking community support, facing domestic violence at home for participating. At the meeting, she met fellow leader **Manju**, who offered personal support and encouragement: *"We will move forward like this; you are not alone."*
  Because Shanti could not read or write, she initially communicated using recorded audio voice notes. Manju urged her to acquire functional literacy: *"We cannot listen to your recordings in front of everyone. Learn to message."* Manju sat with Shanti, teaching her mobile messaging and functional literacy skills. Through persistent effort, Shanti learned to read and write.
- **Intergenerational Educational Breakthrough**:
  Determined that her adopted daughter would access educational opportunities, Shanti enrolled her in the residential **Kasturba Gandhi Balika Vidyalaya (KGBV)**, securing free residential education. Together with Manju, Shanti became among the first mothers from her village to enroll her daughter in KGBV, inspiring other families in the community to support their daughters' formal education.
- **Agro-Ecological Engagement & Civic Facilitation**:
  Shanti became actively involved in farming on her family land, participating in sustainable agricultural practices and homestead vegetable cultivation. Today, Shanti is a confident grassroots facilitator, supporting women facing domestic violence, encouraging participation in collective village platforms, and demonstrating how collective solidarity can rebuild lives shattered by trauma.

### 6.3 Rajkumari Devi: From Wage Labour to High-Yield Turmeric Leadership and Civic Recognition
- **Geographic & Social Identity**: Resident of Kailash Nagar village (Chahalwa GP, Sujauli Police Station, Mihinpurwa Block); husband: Shri Bhikhari; smallholder farmer and active AAS community leader.
- **Prior Agrarian Realities & Wage Labour**:
  Prior to the FASAL intervention, Rajkumari Devi and her family experienced persistent economic insecurity. Although possessing agricultural land, the household practiced conventional farming heavily reliant on expensive synthetic chemical fertilizers, commercial pesticides, and hybrid seeds. Escalating input costs combined with erratic rainfall frequently resulted in low crop margins, compelling Rajkumari Devi and her husband to work as manual agricultural wage laborers to sustain household expenses.
- **Capacity Building and Technical Mastery with DEHAT**:
  Rajkumari Devi’s farming trajectory transformed after joining the Aarti Aajivika Adhikar Sangathan (AAS) in Kailash Nagar. Through DEHAT's agronomic training workshops, she gained technical competencies in:
  - Scientific biological seed treatment using organic bio-inoculants prior to planting.
  - Basal soil enrichment using organic Farm Yard Manure (FYM) and Jeevamrit.
  - Raised-bed planting, geometric line spacing, and protective straw mulching.
  - Strategic crop diversification toward high-value commercial spices.
- **The Turmeric Breakthrough**:
  Allocating a plot measuring less than one Bigha to improved organic turmeric (*Curcuma longa*) intercropped with Colocasia (*Arbi*), Rajkumari Devi applied the complete LEISA package: organic raised beds, regular Jeevamrit drenches, and moisture-conserving straw mulching.
  Her plot achieved outstanding production, harvesting healthy rhizomes that yielded a net profit of approximately **₹60,000** from a sub-bigha plot (recorded in project documentation and Grant Report 2024). This demonstrated to smallholders across Kailash Nagar that organic spice cultivation could deliver strong cash income while avoiding synthetic input debts.
- **Commercial Crop Expansion**:
  Reinvesting agricultural returns, Rajkumari Devi diversified her household farm holdings into multi-year commercial crops:
  - **2 Bighas of commercial Banana cultivation**.
  - **1 Bigha of dedicated organic Turmeric cultivation**.
  These cash crops provided stable household income, reducing reliance on manual wage labor and supporting quality schooling for her children.
- **Civic Engagement and District Recognition**:
  Beyond farm production, Rajkumari Devi became an active civic participant in Kailash Nagar, speaking confidently in Gram Sabhas and encouraging other women farmers to adopt sustainable practices. In recognition of her agricultural accomplishments, she was awarded official **Certificates of Appreciation (*Prashasti Patra*) for Agricultural Excellence** presented by the local Member of Legislative Assembly (MLA) and the District Magistrate (DM) of Bahraich (Grant Report 2024, pp. 9 & 30).

### 6.4 Anarkali: Tribal Community Leadership, PDS Food Rights, and Agro-Ecological Farming
- **Geographic & Social Identity**: Resident of Rampurwa village, Fakirpuri Post, Mihinpurwa Block; member of the indigenous tribal community (*जनजाति*); Treasurer (*Koshadhyaksh*) of **Sadhana Azadi Aajeevika Adhikar Sangathan (AAS)**.
- **Overcoming Social Marginalization and Exclusion**:
  Coming from a marginalized tribal community along the forest periphery, Anarkali grew up in a social environment where tribal families faced structural neglect and discrimination from dominant local groups and public institutions. Tribal women were customarily expected to remain silent, and fear of engaging with government authorities was widespread. Despite these constraints, Anarkali sought avenues to organize community women and defend basic welfare rights.
- **Institutional Role as Treasurer of Sadhana Azadi AAS**:
  Recognizing her dedication, DEHAT organizers supported Anarkali in mobilizing women in Rampurwa, where she was chosen as Treasurer of the *Sadhana Azadi AAS*. Through rights awareness training, she recognized that individual complaints could easily be dismissed, but collective community organization provided the leverage needed to secure public entitlements.
- **Grassroots Entitlement Struggles in Rampurwa**:
  Anarkali participated in several significant rights struggles:
  1. *Public Distribution System (PDS) Food Rights Mobilization*: In Rampurwa and neighboring Fakirpuri hamlets, the local fair-price shop dealer routinely shorted poor families on their food rations, distributing only 3 kg of grain instead of the statutory 5 kg per unit. Anarkali, alongside fellow women leaders, organized community delegations to Tehsil Day, filing formal complaints before senior administrative officials. The resulting official inquiry uncovered widespread distribution irregularities, leading to the dismissal of the corrupt dealer and the restoration of full statutory grain rations to eligible families (FASAL Narrative Report HY2, pp. 8–9; Grant Report 2025, p. 35).
  2. *Advocating for Fair MGNREGA Implementation*: She actively monitored local public works, demanding that job cards be issued to eligible tribal women and wages be disbursed transparently and on schedule.
  3. *Securing PMAY Housing Sanctions*: Overcoming administrative bottlenecks, Anarkali assisted destitute families in filing housing applications, helping secure permanent pucca houses under Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana for multiple poor households.
- **Agro-Ecological Farming & Nutrition Gardens**:
  Anarkali established organic farming practices on her family plot, preparing home-made bio-inputs and developing a diverse homestead kitchen garden (*Poshan Vatika*). Her productive plots demonstrated the viability of chemical-free vegetable and grain production to neighboring farmers across Rampurwa.
- **District-Wide Community Recognition**:
  Today, Anarkali is recognized across Bahraich District as a respected community organizer. Her journey illustrates the transformative potential of grassroots collective organization, where indigenous women become active guardians of constitutional rights and agro-ecological resilience.

### 6.5 Cross-Case Synthesis: Grassroots Agency, Fellow Leadership, and Collective Empowerment
A rigorous comparative analysis across the four biographical profiles reveals vital thematic regularities in the transition from structural marginalization to democratic agency:

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              CROSS-CASE SYNTHESIS: PATHWAYS OF GRASSROOTS LEADERSHIP            │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                                                 │
│   DIMENSION 1: TRANSCENDING STRUCTURAL TRAUMA TO COLLECTIVE CONSCIOUSNESS       │
│   - Manju: Converted childhood deprivation and isolation into school advocacy.   │
│   - Shanti Devi: Overcame child marriage, abandonment, and grief via literacy.  │
│   - Rajkumari Devi: Transitioned from wage labour to high-value cash crops.     │
│   - Anarkali: Transformed tribal exclusion into fearless PDS food governance.   │
│                                                                                 │
│   DIMENSION 2: THE SYNERGY OF ENTITLEMENT STRUGGLE & AGRO-ECOLOGICAL TRANSITION │
│   - In each case, democratic mobilization around tangible rights (PDS rations,  │
│     schools, PMAY housing) preceded and reinforced the confidence needed to     │
│     adopt LEISA practices (Jeevamrit, machan trellises, commercial turmeric).   │
│                                                                                 │
│   DIMENSION 3: PEER MENTORSHIP & INTERGENERATIONAL TRANSFORMATION                │
│   - Mentorship networks (e.g., Manju coaching Shanti Devi in mobile messaging)  │
│     built decentralized social capital independent of external NGO staff.       │
│   - Generational breakthroughs: Prioritizing daughters' residential schooling   │
│     (KGBV) and institutionalizing youth leadership (Bal Sansad/JYF).            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The life trajectories of Manju, Shanti Devi, Rajkumari Devi, and Anarkali demonstrate that the FASAL program’s most durable outcome is not simply the introduction of agricultural techniques, but the cultivation of autonomous, women-led democratic leadership capable of negotiating with the state and leading agrarian transformation.

### 7.2 Human Resource Deployment and Geographic Accountability
Every monthly review presentation opens (Slides 9–10) with an operational mapping of DEHAT’s field team, defining managerial oversight and territorial responsibilities across the target geography:

  - Chief Executive Officer (CEO) / Executive Director: **Devyani Chaturvedi** (recorded in decks as *मुख्य कार्यकारी*, and in Grant Report 2025 as *Executive Director*).
  - Executive Director (ED): **Divyanshu Chaturvedi** (*कार्यकारी निदेशक*).
  - Institution Building, Monitoring & Documentation Lead / Programme Director: **Shwetha M**.
  - Project Manager / Natural Resource Management (NRM) Head: **Ram Narayan Kumar**.
  - Legal & Policy Advisor: **Rameez Ji** (featured through August 2024).

  - Finance & Accounts Officers: **Sowmya Singh** and **Md. Iliyas**.

- **Frontline Community Catalysts (*Samudayik Utprerak*) and Geographic Allocation**:
  To maintain frontline accountability, four community catalysts were assigned territorial responsibility across the 20 intervention villages (Slides 9–10):
  1. **Ashok Kumar**: Accountable for **Chahalwa GP** (5 villages/hamlets: Sirsiyanpurwa, Kailashnagar, Tapra, Bhorahanpurwa, and Mangalpurwa).
  2. **Sami Ahmad**: Accountable for **Fakirpuri GP** (Fakirpuri and Rampurwa), alongside Badhiyanpurwa, Ghurepurwa, and Vishunapur.
  3. **Amar Deep**: Accountable for **Bajpur Bankati GP** and **Barkharia GP** (Dhodhepurwa), alongside Narayan Tanda, Jamuniha, and Vishun Tanda.
  4. **Geeta Prasad**: Accountable for **Karikot GP** (supporting Hazaripurwa, Bhattha Bargadaha, Azamgarhpurwa, Lohra, and Rajaram Tanda).

### 7.3 Behavioral Transformation Paradigm: *Chali Aa Rahi Soch* vs. *Ab Ka Soch*
A central qualitative methodology embedded in the monthly review presentations (Slides 13–14 across unique decks) is the structured tracking of **Behavioral Change (*Vyavahar Parivartan*) across 1,500 Families**. Rather than evaluating progress solely through physical input tallies, DEHAT monitored collective shifts in community consciousness, contrasting traditional dependency attitudes (*Chali Aa Rahi Soch*) with progressive democratic agency (*Ab Ka Soch*):

| Behavioral Dimension | Traditional Dependency Thinking (*Chali Aa Rahi Soch*) | Progressive Collective Consciousness (*Ab Ka Soch*) |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Ownership of Collective Organization** | Viewing the organization as belonging to an external NGO (*"संगठन दूसरे किसी संस्था का है"*); attending meetings only if individually invited (*"नेवता देने पर बैठना"*). | Collective ownership: *"The organization belongs to us, it is ours"* (*"संगठन हमलोगों का है, हमारा है"*); assembling voluntarily on scheduled dates. |
| **2. Relationship with State & Bureaucracy** | Passive deference: viewing politicians and officials as paternalistic providers (*"नेताजी + पदाधिकारी माई-बाप"*); relying solely on voting. | Democratic constitutional agency: recognizing that community solidarity (*"समुदाय सबकुछ"*) holds the authority to claim public rights. |
| **3. Gender Equality & Family Valuation** | Patriarchal preference: valuing sons over daughters; treating sons as the sole heirs of family lineage (*"बेटा वंश का चिराग"*). | Gender equality: treating sons and daughters with equal respect (*"बेटा-बेटी समानता"*); viewing both as equal bearers of family lineage (*"दोनों वंश का चिराग"*). |
| **4. Substance Abuse & Social Norms** | Normalizing alcoholism as customary village habit (*"नशा एक कुप्रचलन / स्थानीय शोषक द्वारा आदत में लाना"*). | Collective community action: discussing alcoholism within AAS platforms and enforcing social boycotts (*"आस में चर्चा कर वहिष्कार"*). |
| **5. Children’s Education** | Neglecting formal schooling; tolerating child manual labor in fields and domestic chores (*"बच्चों की पढ़ाई पर ध्यान नहीं"*). | Prioritizing universal education: taking parental responsibility for schooling (*"पढ़ाई की मुख्य जबाबदेही"*); opposing child labor. |
| **6. Agronomic Practice** | Exclusive reliance on chemical monocultures (*"धान-गेहूं-मेंथा–गन्ना; रासायनिक खाद दवा"*); purchasing expensive synthetic inputs. | Agro-ecological transition: adopting diversified vegetables, multilayer farming, and medicinal crops (*"सब्जी, बहुस्तरीय – औषधीय"*); producing biological composts. |

### 7.4 Monthly Governance and Institutional Rhythms
The monthly decks record the actual governance cadence achieved across the three-tier federation:
- **AAS Hamlet Meetings**: Monthly targets were established at 80 meetings (through August 2024) and 74 meetings (from September 2024 onward, reflecting intentional operational consolidation). **Achieved monthly meetings ranged between 12 and 68 meetings (16% to 92% operational achievement)**. Meeting frequency dropped significantly during severe monsoon inundation (e.g., July 2024: 32 of 80 [40.0%]; August 2024: 12 of 74 [16.2%]; June 2025: 38 of 74 [51.4%]), recovering during dry winter and spring months (e.g., April 2025: 68 of 74 [91.9%]; May 2025: 68 of 74 [91.9%]).
- **Cluster Federation Meetings**: Monthly targets were set at 4 to 5 cluster meetings, with 1 to 4 assemblies typically achieved depending on seasonal agricultural workloads.
- **Sangharsh Block Federation**: Convened periodically at the block level for administrative interfaces, with meetings suspended during peak agricultural seasons (such as August through November 2025).
- **Krishi Mitra Cadre Meetings**: Organized monthly at village nodes (such as Fakirpuri, Jamuniha, and Rajaram Tanda) to review seasonal crop practices and biological pest management.

### 7.5 Agronomic Seasonality, Crop Cycles, and Input Dissemination
The slide presentations illustrate the seasonal rhythm of field interventions across the 22 reviewed months:
- **Winter (January – February)**: Digging, cleaning, and aggregating mature turmeric crops (e.g., January 2025 Deck S15: 17 Bighas harvested, yielding 230 quintals of fresh rhizomes); distribution of plastic drums for Jeevamrit brewing; establishing raised nursery beds for summer cucurbits and onion seedlings.
- **Spring (March – April)**: Harvesting of Rabi lentils, mustard, and wheat; distribution of seed kits for summer Zaid vegetables; nursery transplantation of chillies and tomatoes; preparation of botanical sprays.
- **Summer (May – June)**: Pre-monsoon field preparation; distribution of mother turmeric and ginger rhizomes; green manuring promotion; construction and repair of bamboo-and-wire *Machan* trellises.
- **Monsoon (July – September)**: Line transplantation of Kharif paddy; managing flood drainage in low-lying plots; foliar application of botanical bio-pest sprays (*Neemastra* and *Char Chatni*) against defoliators; manual weeding and mulching of turmeric beds.
- **Autumn (October – December)**: Paddy harvesting and post-harvest threshing; land preparation for winter wheat, mustard, and garlic; establishment of homestead kitchen gardens (*Poshan Vatikas*) with winter greens; preparation of annual grant progress reports.

### 7.6 Public Convergence, Jan Sunvayi (1076), and Administrative Accountability
The presentations provide continuous documentation of public scheme convergence:
- **Subsidized Borewell Sanctions**: The January 2025 presentation (Deck 11, Slide 15) records the administrative processing and approval of **352 subsidized borewell applications** (*"बोरिंग पास हुए आवेदन 352"*) submitted in 2024. In subsequent review periods, actual input deliveries proceeded in batches (e.g., September 2025 Deck S15 noting borings distributed to 8 farmers).
- **Grievance Redressal via CM Helpline 1076**: Facilitators supported community members in utilizing the Chief Minister’s online grievance portal (1076) to report non-functional infrastructure and service delays. Complaint volumes recorded across four 2025 review decks ranged between 2 and 7 specific complaints per month. While systematic grievance resolution rates were not compiled in the slide decks, specific successes were highlighted, such as the installation of a drinking water handpump following a 1076 grievance petition (May 2025 Deck, Slide 47).
- **Border Area Coordination (SSB Engagement)**: Decks document structured community interface meetings with border security forces stationed along the international boundary (e.g., May 2024 Deck S23: *"SSB 79 Rampurwa ke saath baithak"*; February 2024 Deck S72: *"SSB Rampurwa Battalion 76... human trafficking prevention"*), fostering coordination on anti-trafficking monitoring and community security.

### 7.7 Organizational SWOT Dynamics Across Implementation Months
A recurring component of monthly review meetings (featured in 21 of the 22 unique presentations, typically on Slides 19–21) is the transparent **SWOT Analysis (Strengths, Weaknesses, Opportunities, Threats)** conducted by the field team:
- **Strengths (*Shakti*)**: Strong, organic connection with local communities (*स्थानीय समुदाय से जुड़ाव*); active participation and leadership of women farmers (*महिला किसान की अधिक से अधिक भागीदारी*); knowledgeable and multidisciplinary field team (*विषय के जानकार टीम*); growing collaboration with government line departments (Agriculture and Horticulture).
- **Weaknesses (*Kamjori*)**: Personal, environmental, and geographic stress experienced by frontline staff operating in isolated border terrains (*व्यक्तिगत एवं परिवेश की समस्याओं से बना तनाव*); gaps in administrative and financial documentation among newly appointed village workers (*कार्यकर्ताओं में आर्थिक जबाबदेही का अभाव*).
- **Opportunities (*Avasar*)**: Leveraging available public resources and subsidies through government agricultural and horticultural development schemes; expanding community-level value addition.
- **Threats (*Khatra*)**: Misuse of official authority and legal intimidation by lower forestry personnel (*कानून का दुरूपयोग*); exploitation by exploitative middlemen (*बिचौलिया*); lack of fair market pricing for organic produce in conventional markets (*बाजार में जैविक उत्पादों को सही मूल्य न मिलना*); climatic instability and crop loss from erratic weather events (*अनिश्चित मौसम से किसान को हानि*).

### 7.8 Media Engagement, Local Press Coverage, and Public Accountability
In addition to internal monitoring, the monthly presentations document active engagement with regional print media to ensure public accountability and administrative transparency:
- **Coverage of Administrative Interface Assemblies**: Slide decks document extensive regional press coverage of community interface meetings (e.g., December 2025 Deck, Slide 22, showcasing *Dainik Jagran* coverage: *"दैनिक जागरण के नजर में जिला स्तरीय इंटरफेस"*). Regional newspapers reported on citizen delegations presenting memoranda on road connectivity, drinking water, and school facilities directly to district authorities.
- **Village-Level Child Protection & Entitlement News**: Slide decks archive media clippings documenting village-level assemblies, including Village-Level Child Protection Committee (VLCPC) meetings (e.g., June 2025 Deck, Slide 42, *"VLCPC बैठक की न्यूज"*), highlighting community monitoring of child safety, trafficking prevention along the Nepal border, and fair implementation of welfare schemes.
- **Amplifying Grassroots Voice**: Local press coverage served as an external accountability mechanism, ensuring that administrative commitments made by line departments during interface meetings entered the public record and could be tracked by community federations.

## Part 8: Quantitative Microdata Architecture & Thematic Domain Schemas

### 8.1 The Primary Survey Pipeline & Relational Taxonomy
The empirical foundation of the FASAL monitoring and evaluation system rests upon a multi-stage data collection, enumeration, and digitization pipeline designed to document the socio-ecological conditions of forest-fringe farming households. Data originated through the paper-based **Family Development Booklet (FDB)** (*परिवार विकास पुस्तिका*), a structured 16-page physical household tracking instrument administered in Hindi by community resource persons (Krishi Mitras) and field coordinators.

Crucially, methodological auditing of submission timestamps and survey architecture establishes that the household dataset does not represent three independent longitudinal panel waves. All 1,329 households were enumerated in a **single cross-sectional retrospective recall survey round** administered between 6 February 2025 and 3 November 2025 (with three pilot questionnaires initiated in July 2024). All submissions were uploaded through a centralized enumerator account (`shwetha05`), with a median form completion/transcription duration of approximately 296 minutes. Retrospective recall was utilized to collect multi-year data covering three agricultural production cycles (2022, 2023, and 2024) and six half-year recall blocks (January–June 2022 through July–December 2024).

The field instruments were transcribed and uploaded via Kobo Collect / OpenRosa XML into KoboToolbox, subsequently undergoing structural extraction into tabular databases:

```
[Paper FDB Registry] (16-Page Physical Household Instrument Administered in 20 Villages)
       │
       ▼
[Kobo Collect OpenRosa XML Mobile Engine] (Single Retrospective Survey Round, Feb–Nov 2025)
       │
       ▼
[Raw KoboToolbox Server Export] ───► Main Household Sheet: 1,330 rows × 4,467 columns
       │                               (1 Header Row + 1,329 Valid Household Submissions)
       │                            Repeat Member Sheet: 5,797 rows × 19 columns
       │                               (1 Header Row + 5,796 Individual Resident Members)
       │
       │ (Algorithmic Deduplication Defect: Last-Copy Overwrite & Column Dropping)
       ▼
[Standardized FDB Dataset: DEHAT_Cleaned_Data.csv] ───► 1,329 rows × 2,999 base variables
       │                                                   (1,468 RAW Columns Discarded)
       │
       │ (Feature Engineering: Undisclosed Multiplier of 0.20 Acre / Local Bigha)
       ▼
[Analytical Dataset: DEHAT_Cleaned_Data_with_Acres.csv] ──► 1,329 rows × 3,002 variables
       │
       ├──► 2025 Horticultural Production Register (`Updated_Fasal Crop wise details_Update _2025.xlsx`)
       ├──► Multi-Year Public Convergence Workbooks (2023, 2024, 2025 Primary Registers)
       ├──► Citizen Service Centre (Jan Suvidha Kendra) Register (1,102 Genuine Records)
       └──► FASAL Capacity Building & Training MIS (210 Conducted Sessions, 13,510 Attendances)
```

### 8.2 Quantitative Audit of the Functional FDB Domains (3,002 Variables)
Algorithmic inspection of `DEHAT_Cleaned_Data_with_Acres.csv` (N=1,329 households) reveals exactly 3,002 columns. A widespread error in earlier documentation was the assertion of 11 domains with column counts that summed to 3,010, including an entirely fictitious "D07 Institutional Governance" domain of 8 columns. In truth, zero institutional governance columns exist in the household dataset; governance indicators exist exclusively in the child member roster (`family_member_repeat`).

The true, reproducible column distribution across functional thematic domains in `DEHAT_Cleaned_Data_with_Acres.csv` strictly sums to 3,002:

```

+--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

+----------+------------------------------------------+--------------+--------------------+---------------------------------------------+----------+----------+

| --       | Half-Year Temporal Survey Flags          |     6        | 6 Half-Years       | Indicator flags for active recall blocks    | 100.0%   | 100.0%   |
| D03      | Agronomic Production & Crop Economics    |   390        | 3 Annual Cycles    | 387 crop economics cols + 3 confirm flags   |  35.7%   | 100.0%   |

| D07      | Institutional & Collective Governance    |     0        | None               | [Fictitious domain; absent from FDB schema] |    --    |    --    |
| D08      | Survey Metadata & Cryptographic Tags     |    13        | Administrative     | Submission timestamps, Surveyor, UUIDs      |  76.9%   | 100.0%   |
| D09      | Administrative Geography & Location      |     5        | Administrative     | District, Block, GP, Village, AAS Code      |  62.4%   | 100.0%   |
| D10      | Landholding Baseline (Local Bigha)       |     3        | Retrospective Rec. | Owned, Leased, and Total Land (Raw Bigha)   |  86.8%   |  87.3%   |
| D11      | Engineered Landholding Acreage (Acres)   |     3        | Derived (0.20 ac/b)| Owned, Leased, and Total Land (Derived Acres)| 86.8%   |  87.3%   |
+----------+------------------------------------------+--------------+--------------------+---------------------------------------------+----------+----------+
| TOTAL    | Exhaustive Analytical FDB Database       | 3,002        | 2022–2024 Lifecycle| Complete Empirical Household Feature Set    |  77.4%   | 100.0%   |
+--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

### 8.3 Deconstructing the Raw KoboToolbox Proliferation (4,467 Columns)
A comparative architectural audit between the raw digital Kobo export (`Family_Development_Book_..._2026-06-22-14-05-16.xlsx`, 4,467 columns) and the consolidated analytical CSV (3,002 columns) reveals the computational mechanisms governing field data inflation and severe pipeline data loss:
1. **XLSForm Cascading Multipliers**: In the Kobo OpenRosa data model, dependent questions generate unique columnar branches for every conditional option in a cascade. In the geographical roster, the selection of `Village Panchayat` branched into **7 discrete `Village Name` columns** (one per GP cascade, which together cover 100% of the 1,329 households), and the selection of `Village` branched into **20 discrete `AAS Name` columns** (18 non-empty). Across these columns, exactly 18 distinct villages and 66 AAS groups are recorded.
2. **Multi-Select Array Expansion**: Dietary tracking questions stored both a concatenated string variable and individual binary indicator flags (e.g., `/greens`, `/ladyfinger`, `/banana`, `/Other`), expanding 36 monthly queries into 1,260 distinct columns (36 months × 35 columns per month = 3 text headers + 32 discrete food item flags).
3. **The Data Cleaning Pipeline Defect (SYS-1)**: During the programmatic generation of `DEHAT_Cleaned_Data.xlsx` (and the derived CSVs), the cleaning script encountered 404 repeated question labels across survey cycles. Rather than merging or uniquely renaming them, the deduplication logic retained **only the last physical occurrence** of each label and discarded **1,468 columns**. Crucially, the surviving column kept the positional header of the *first* occurrence:
   - For longitudinal modules (migration, livestock, scheme awareness), columns labeled "Jan–Jun 2022" actually contain Jul–Dec 2024 data, producing severe temporal dislocation.
   - For agricultural economics, **30 crop net-income columns** (10 per year: Rabi pea, coriander, garlic, onion; Zaid onion, cucumber, bitter gourd, cabbage, beetroot; groundnut) were completely dropped, erasing ₹22.28 Lakh in 2022, ₹36.33 Lakh in 2023, and ₹93.72 Lakh in 2024.
   - For administrative geography, only the last GP cascade column (Mangalpurwa, 88 households) was retained, leaving `Village Name` empty in 1,241 out of 1,329 rows (93.4% null) in the CSV, even though the raw Kobo data has 100% complete village coverage.
4. **Administrative Distribution Across Gram Panchayats**: While project narratives frequently refer to "6 Gram Panchayats", the household microdata records exactly **7 Gram Panchayats**:
   - **Karikot GP**: 602 Households (45.30%)
   - **Fakirpuri GP**: 229 Households (17.23%)
   - **Chahalwa GP**: 183 Households (13.77%)
   - **Bajpur Bankati GP**: 113 Households (8.50%)
   - **Mangalpurwa GP**: 88 Households (6.62%)
   - **Vishunapur GP**: 73 Households (5.49%)
   - **Badkhadiya GP**: 41 Households (3.09%)

### 8.4 The Individual Family Member Roster (`family_member_repeat`: N=5,796)
Sheet 2 of the raw Kobo export comprises a relational child table containing **5,796 individual resident family members** (5,797 rows including the header), linked to the 1,329 primary households through foreign keys `_submission__uuid` and `_parent_index`. Exactly 12 member rows lack a personal name.

```
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| RELATIONAL ROSTER SCHEMA: family_member_repeat (5,796 Individuals Linked to 1,329 Households)                                    |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Relational Key / Variable Header      | Data Type | Description and Functional Purpose                                          |
+---------------------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| `Name of Family Member`               | Text      | Full name of individual household member (2,915 Devanagari, 2,866 Latin)    |
| `Relationship with family head`       | Text      | 11 Relations: Son, Daughter, Wife, Mother, Brother, Father, In-laws, etc.   |
| `Is this member part of Bal Sansad?`  | Boolean   | Participation in Children's Parliament (50 Yes, 5,669 No, 77 Null)          |
| `Is this member part of Kishor Sang.?`| Boolean   | Participation in Adolescent Collectives (7 Yes, 5,700 No, 89 Null)          |
| `Is this member part of Yuva Sansad?` | Boolean   | Participation in Youth Parliament (10 Yes, 5,701 No, 85 Null)               |
| `Is member part of AAS?`              | Boolean   | Membership in Aajeevika Adhikar Sangathan (141 Yes, 5,575 No, 80 Null)      |
| `Any other groups member is part of?` | Text      | Text responses (Top: 'No' 1,447; 'नहीं' 855; 'Dehat jay ajadi' 82)          |
| `_parent_index`                       | Integer   | 1-based index matching row index in parent household master sheet (1–1,329) |
| `_submission__uuid`                   | UUID-128  | Cryptographic foreign key matching `_uuid` in parent household survey       |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

Cross-sectional tabulation of group participation in this sub-roster establishes:
- **Ajeevika Adhikar Sangathan (AAS)**: 141 members flagged with general collective membership (not "executive delegates").
- **Bal Sansad (Children's Parliament)**: 50 active child participants across 40 unique households.
- **Yuva Sansad (Youth Parliament)**: 10 youth delegates.
- **Kishor Sangathan (Adolescent Collectives)**: 7 adolescent members.

**Critical Data Boundary Notice**: In earlier drafts of the synthesis, narrative claims asserted the presence of specific age brackets (e.g. "ages 6–14", "ages 15–18", "ages 19–29"), digital literacy metrics, and memberships in "Self-Help Groups, Village Forest Protection Committees, and Water Sanitation Committees". Empirical inspection confirms that **zero demographic variables** (no age, sex, educational attainment, or marital status) exist in `family_member_repeat`. Furthermore, string searches in the "Any other groups" column reveal zero occurrences of SHG, forest protection, or water committees.

## Part 9: Landholding Distribution & Tenurial Structures

### 9.1 Aggregate Acreage, Central Tendencies & Conversion Factors
Landholdings in the Katarniaghat forest fringe are characterized by severe micro-fragmentation. Out of 1,329 surveyed households, exactly **1,160 households** have non-null agricultural land records (169 households have missing/null land measurements, and 36 households report exactly 0.00 acres).

In the raw survey form, land areas were recorded as free text in local units (e.g., *"2 बीघा"*, *"1vigha"*, *"10 vishuva"*). During pipeline construction, the analytical columns `Total_Land_(Acres)`, `Owned_Land_(Acres)`, and `Leased_Land_(Acres)` were generated by applying an **undisclosed conversion factor of 0.20 acre per local bigha** (5 bigha = 1 acre; 1 biswa = 0.01 acre). 

Excluding the transcription anomaly for farmer "[Redacted Farmer Name]" (Row index 984, `_index` 985, Vishunapur GP), where a Krishi Mitra contact number `[REDACTED-PHONE]` was accidentally entered into the owned bigha field (generating a spurious 1.93 billion acres), the aggregate operational farmland across all 1,160 reporting households is **886.04 acres**. 

At the operational factor of 0.20 acre/bigha, 886.04 acres represents **4,430.2 local bigha** (mean: 3.82 bigha; median: 2.50 bigha). The figure of "1,431.4 bigha" cited in prior literature was back-calculated by dividing 886.04 acres by standard UP pucca bigha (0.619 acre/bigha)—a factor that was never utilized in the data pipeline.

```
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| VERIFIED PARAMETRIC & NON-PARAMETRIC LAND DISTRIBUTION (N=1,160 Households, 886.04 Total Acres @ 0.20 Ac/Bigha)                  |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Statistical Metric                    | Total Land (Acres)     | Owned Land (Acres)*    | Leased Land (Acres)    | Local Bigha Equiv. |
+---------------------------------------+------------------------+------------------------+------------------------+--------------------+
| Reporting Households (Non-Null)       | 1,160                  | 1,158                  | 1,143                  | 1,160              |
| Sum Operational Area (Acres)          | 886.04                 | 871.82                 | 133.74                 | 4,430.20 Bigha     |
| Arithmetic Mean (Acres / Household)   | 0.7638 (0.764)         | 0.7529 (0.753)         | 0.1170 (0.115 w/zeros) | 3.819 Bigha        |
| Median (50th Percentile)              | 0.5000 (0.500)         | 0.5000 (0.500)         | 0.0000 (0.000)         | 2.500 Bigha        |
| Standard Deviation (Sample, ddof=1)   | 0.8298                 | 0.8302                 | 0.4107 (0.408 w/zeros) | 4.149 Bigha        |
| Minimum Recorded Value                | 0.0000 (36 Households) | 0.0000                 | 0.0000                 | 0.000 Bigha        |
| Maximum Recorded Value                | 8.0000                 | 8.0000                 | 5.0000                 | 40.00 Bigha        |
| 10th Percentile (p10)                 | 0.2000                 | 0.2000                 | 0.0000                 | 1.000 Bigha        |
| 25th Percentile (p25 / Q1)            | 0.2500                 | 0.2000                 | 0.0000                 | 1.250 Bigha        |
| 75th Percentile (p75 / Q3)            | 1.0000                 | 1.0000                 | 0.0000                 | 5.000 Bigha        |
| 90th Percentile (p90)                 | 1.8200                 | 1.8000                 | 0.4000                 | 9.100 Bigha        |
| 95th Percentile (p95)                 | 2.4000                 | 2.4000                 | 0.6000                 | 12.00 Bigha        |
| 99th Percentile (p99)                 | 4.0000                 | 4.0000                 | 2.0000                 | 20.00 Bigha        |
| Interquartile Range (IQR = Q3 - Q1)   | 0.7500 Acres           | 0.8000 Acres           | 0.0000 Acres           | 3.750 Bigha        |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
```
*\* Note: Owned land excludes row index 984. Earlier reports cited 752.30 acres for owned land because they calculated Owned as `Total − Leased` (886.04 − 133.74 = 752.30), which is mathematically invalid as owned and leased land do not sum to total land.*

**Critical Arithmetic Inconsistency (SYS-6 / A08)**:
In `DEHAT_Cleaned_Data_with_Acres.csv`, the relationship $\text{Owned} + \text{Leased} = \text{Total}$ is violated in **176 out of 1,142 households** with complete data:
- In **169 households**, $\text{Owned} + \text{Leased} > \text{Total}$, caused by enumerators recording the total holding redundantly in both owned and leased fields (e.g. entering "2 bigha" for owned, "2 bigha" for leased, and "2 bigha" for total).
- In **7 households**, $\text{Owned} + \text{Leased} < \text{Total}$.
- Across all reporting households, actual owned land sums to **871.82 acres** and leased land sums to **133.74 acres**, yielding a combined sum of **1,005.56 acres**, which exceeds total recorded land (886.04 acres) by 119.52 acres.

### 9.2 Tenurial Typologies & Tenancy Regimes
Cross-tabulation of owned versus leased holdings establishes four primary operational tenurial categories:
1. **Pure Owner-Cultivators (N=954 Households, 82.2%)**: Cultivate only titled or ancestral land (leased land = 0.0). They control **750.07 acres of Total Land** (mean: 0.786 acres; owned land sum: 757.45 acres).
2. **Mixed Owner-Tenants (N=160 Households, 13.8%)**: Own marginal plots (mean owned: 0.715 acres) but lease in additional land (mean leased: 0.730 acres; leased sum: 116.72 acres) to achieve operational viability. Their total operational area averages 0.779 acres per household (124.67 acres total). (Counting the phone-number row brings this to 161 households and 117.72 leased acres).
3. **Pure Tenants / Sharecroppers (N=24 Households, 2.1%)**: Own 0.00 acres of titled land (excluding the corrupted phone row). They lease in **16.02 acres** (mean: 0.668 acres; range: 0.02 to 2.00 acres).
4. **Recorded Zero-Land Households (N=36 Households, 3.1%)**: Report exactly 0.00 acres of Total Land (21 of these explicitly report 0.00 in both owned and leased sub-fields).
5. **The Displaced / Unrecorded Cultivator Cohort (N=163 Households)**: Exactly **163 households** have zero or missing land records in the land module (129 with missing land, 34 with zero land) but report positive net crop income in the agricultural module, earning between ₹8 and ₹2,08,000 annually. These represent households cultivating unrecorded forest-edge plots, homestead kitchen spaces, or informal unwritten seasonal sharecropping arrangements.

```
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| STRATIFICATION BY OPERATIONAL FARM SIZE CATEGORY (N=1,160 Reporting Households)                                                  |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Farm Size Category          | Operational Range      | Households (N) | Percentage (%) | Aggregate Area (Ac) | Mean Area (Ac/HH) |
+-----------------------------+------------------------+----------------+----------------+---------------------+-------------------+
| Landless / Homestead Only   | Exactly 0.00 Acres     |    36          |   3.10%        |     0.00            |  0.000            |
| Sub-Marginal Farms          | > 0.00 to <= 0.50 Acres|   558          |  48.10%        |   164.16            |  0.294            |
| Marginal Farms              | > 0.50 to <= 1.00 Acre |   372          |  32.07%        |   291.98            |  0.785            |
| Small Farms                 | > 1.00 to <= 2.00 Acres|   127          |  10.95%        |   206.50            |  1.626            |
| Semi-Medium Farms           | > 2.00 to <= 4.00 Acres|    56          |   4.83%        |   164.20            |  2.932            |
| Medium Farms                | > 4.00 to <= 8.00 Acres|    11          |   0.95%        |    59.20            |  5.382            |
+-----------------------------+------------------------+----------------+----------------+---------------------+-------------------+
| TOTAL OPERATIONAL UNIVERSE  | All Land Categories    | 1,160          | 100.00%        |   886.04            |  0.764            |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
```
**Analytical Takeaway**: Exactly **83.3% of reporting households** operate on 1.00 acre or less (80.2% of non-zero holders), and **51.2% operate on half an acre or less**.

### 9.3 Comprehensive Bigha Conversion Sensitivity Analysis
Because agricultural productivity per acre is calculated as $\text{Income} \div \text{Acres}$, adopting a conversion factor of $f$ acre/bigha scales all per-acre productivity figures by $\frac{0.20}{f}$. 

To provide absolute empirical transparency, the table below documents the sensitivity of total landholding, mean holding, 2024 per-acre income, and the programmatic ₹60,000/acre target attainment rate across standard revenue definitions of bigha:
- **0.2000 ac/bigha**: Local Eastern UP / Bahraich informal bigha (5 bigha = 1 acre; the factor embedded in the pipeline).
- **0.2500 ac/bigha**: Standard Eastern UP Kachcha Bigha (4 bigha = 1 acre).
- **0.3333 ac/bigha**: Standard 3 bigha / acre definition.
- **0.5000 ac/bigha**: 2 bigha / acre definition.
- **0.6190 / 0.6250 ac/bigha**: Standard UP Government Pucca Bigha (3,025 sq. yards; 1 acre = 1.6 to 1.615 bigha).

```
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| COMPREHENSIVE BIGHA CONVERSION SENSITIVITY MATRIX (N=1,160 Landholders; N=1,122 Positive Income & Land > 0)                                       |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Bigha Conversion Factor (f)  | Total Land | Mean Land | Median Land| CSV Mean ₹/Ac| CSV % ≥₹60k/ac | RAW % ≥₹60k/ac | 2025 Veg Income/Acre (₹)   |
|                              | (Acres)    | (Acres)   | (Acres)    | (2024 Endline)| (2024 Endline) | (2024 Endline) | (₹1.70 Cr ÷ Total Land)    |
+------------------------------+------------+-----------+------------+--------------+----------------+----------------+----------------------------+
| **0.2000 ac/bigha (Pipeline)**| **886.0**  | **0.764** | **0.500**  | **₹90,578**  | **68.2% (765)**| **75.0% (842)**| **₹19,162**                |
| **0.2500 ac/bigha (Kachcha)** | 1,107.6    |  0.955    |   0.625    |   ₹72,463    |  48.8% (547)   |  55.8% (626)   |   ₹15,330                  |
| **0.3333 ac/bigha (3/acre)**  | 1,476.6    |  1.273    |   0.833    |   ₹54,352    |  24.9% (280)   |  30.4% (341)   |   ₹11,498                  |
| **0.5000 ac/bigha (2/acre)**  | 2,215.1    |  1.910    |   1.250    |   ₹36,231    |   8.8% (99)    |  12.2% (137)   |    ₹7,665                  |
| **0.6190 ac/bigha (UP Pucca)**| 2,742.3    |  2.364    |   1.548    |   ₹29,266    |   5.8% (65)    |   8.1% (91)    |    ₹6,191                  |
| **0.6250 ac/bigha (5/8 ac)**  | 2,768.9    |  2.387    |   1.563    |   ₹28,985    |   5.8% (65)    |   7.9% (89)    |    ₹6,132                  |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

**Decisive Methodological Finding**:
The claim that "68.2% of farming households exceeded the APPI ₹60,000/acre target in 2024" is **wholly dependent on the 0.20 acre/bigha assumption**. Under standard UP Pucca Bigha (0.619 ac/bigha), 2024 attainment drops precipitously to **5.8% on CSV data (65 households)** and **8.1% on RAW data (91 households)**. Under standard Eastern UP Kachcha Bigha (0.25 ac/bigha), attainment is **48.8% on CSV (547 households)** and **55.8% on RAW (626 households)**. Publication standards require reporting all figures alongside this sensitivity framework.

## Part 10: Longitudinal Crop Economics & Agrarian Performance (2022–2025)

### 10.1 Multi-Year Net Crop Income Progression & Baseline Rectification
The foundational objective of the FASAL program was to enable marginalized smallholders to graduate from baseline agricultural subsistence (~₹10,000/acre) toward ₹60,000/acre/year through agro-ecological transition and diversification.

However, prior synthesis claims of a **"3.39-fold expansion"** (from ₹14,554 in 2022 to ₹49,328 in 2024) were heavily distorted by two systemic data-cleaning defects in `DEHAT_Cleaned_Data_with_Acres.csv`:
1. **The 2022 Paddy Net Income Overwrite Defect (SYS-2)**: In the raw Kobo export, 2022 paddy sales totaled ₹2,31,72,450 and costs totaled ₹85,22,800, generating a true positive net income of **+₹1,58,40,719** across 1,082 normal households. In the cleaned CSV, the column `...net income...-paddy in 2022` was overwritten with **negative cost (`−cost`) across all 1,136 non-null rows**, summing to **−₹85,12,800**. This single defect artificially subtracted **₹2.44 Crore** from the 2022 baseline income.
2. **The 30 Dropped Crop Columns Defect (SYS-1)**: Deduplication of repeated question labels discarded 10 major crop slots per year, erasing ₹22.28 Lakh in 2022, ₹36.33 Lakh in 2023, and ₹93.72 Lakh in 2024.

Recomputing all crop revenues and costs directly from the raw Kobo export restores the true empirical progression:


**Key Empirical Insights**:
1. **True Growth Trajectory (1.63× vs 3.39×)**: Household net crop income grew by **1.63× (+63.1%)**, rising from ₹34,555.01 to ₹56,377.16. This aligns with the external Midline Evaluation report (`Midline_FASAL_Jaykar (1).docx`), which documented an income expansion from ₹32,983 to ₹54,993 (+66.7%).
2. **The ₹70 Crore Outlier (Row index 1206)**: In 2024 raw data, farmer "[Redacted Farmer Name]" (Fakirpuri GP, row index 1206) carries an entry of **₹70,00,75,173** in 2024 Tomato net income. When evaluating 2024 parametric statistics, this cell must be excluded, reducing aggregate 2024 crop income from ₹76.57 Crore to ₹7.49 Crore.
3. **Deconstructing the "Elite 108"**: The claim that exactly 108 households earned ≥₹60,000/acre in all three years arose from an intermediate script (`fasal_program_analysis.py`) that merged 34 distinct households sharing the same name and father's name. On a true per-household basis, the count is **81 households** on the CSV data, and **319 households** on the corrected RAW data (at 0.20 ac/bigha).

### 10.2 Biographical Case Study Corrections
Verification of individual farmer profiles highlighted in prior literature resolves several factual errors:
- **Reeta Devi (W/o Bhagiram)**: Operates 0.12 acres. Her true Gram Panchayat is **Fakirpuri** (not Karikot). Her verified RAW net income expanded from ₹88,000 (2022) to ₹1,02,200 (2023) and ₹1,38,600 (2024) (CSV income: ₹55,500 / ₹88,000 / ₹1,22,500). Her main income drivers are wheat, paddy, mustard, and maize.
- **Dongli (W/o Mannu Ram)**: Operates 0.10 acres in **Fakirpuri** (not Karikot). Her net income rose from ₹24,300 (2022) to ₹49,700 (2023) and ₹68,900 (2024), driven by wheat, paddy, potato, and turmeric. Trellis bitter gourd and machan cultivation are absent from her records.
- **Bindo (W/o Chhote)**: Operates 0.20 acres (1 bigha). While generating >₹2,00,000 annually, **70% to 83% of her revenue is driven by sugarcane** (₹1.50 to ₹1.80 Lakh), representing a high-value commercial outlier rather than vegetable nursery polyculture.
- **Astuni (अस्तुनी)**: Operates 0.60 acres. Her verified net income trajectory was ₹1,19,103 (2022) → ₹36,700 (2023) → ₹50,800 (2024). Rather than "expanding", her income experienced a sharp 59% decline from 2022 to 2024 following an exceptional initial wheat harvest.

### 10.3 Crop-Specific Economics & Restoration of Dropped Modules
Recomputation from raw microdata corrects major crop-specific findings:
- **Paddy (*Oryza sativa*)**: Grown by 1,082 to 1,093 households. Aggregate net income was **₹1,58,40,719 in 2022 (RAW)**, ₹1.70 Crore in 2023, and ₹1.87 Crore in 2024.
- **Bitter Gourd (*Momordica charantia*)**: Prior synthesis reported only 221 growers and ₹2.66 Lakh in 2024 because the cleaning pipeline discarded the Zaid bitter gourd column. In the raw data, the dropped Zaid column contains **1,047 growers** generating ₹14.08 Lakh; across both slots, bitter gourd engaged **1,059 unique growers** generating **₹16.74 Lakh**. The narrative of an "expansion to 902 growers in 2025" was an artifact of comparing the incomplete CSV column against the 2025 register.
- **Kharif Pulses & Groundnut**: Groundnut is an oilseed (*Arachis hypogaea*) grown by 39 to 62 farmers in the restored raw data (generating ₹6,944 to ₹7,621/acre). Moong had 5 to 6 active growers.
- **Wheat (*Triticum aestivum*)**: Grown by 1,093 to 1,097 households, generating ₹1.86 Crore (2023) and ₹2.17 Crore (2024). A secondary wheat column (106–118 growers, ₹11.5–19.0 Lakh) represents winter border intercropping.
- **Verified Horticultural Crops (2024)**: Potato: 212 growers, ₹9.76 Lakh; Mustard: 266 growers, ₹11.78 Lakh; Okra: 968 growers, ₹13.46 Lakh; Cowpea: 480 growers, ₹6.64 Lakh; Zaid Tomato: 671 growers (including Row 1206 outlier).
- **Crop Diversification Index (CDI)**: Across all 1,329 households, mean crop varieties cultivated per household were 5.96 (2022), 8.81 (2023), and 10.80 (2024). In the raw Kobo data, these are 8.64 (2022), 11.47 (2023), and 13.93 (2024). The botanical spectrum encompasses **~38 distinct crops** (41 case-insensitive text labels).

## Part 11: Master 2025 Agricultural Portfolio & Horticultural Crop Economics

### 11.1 Macro Production & Commercialization Balance Sheet
In 2025, DEHAT instituted a dedicated seasonal field ledger (`Updated_Fasal Crop wise details_Update _2025.xlsx`, sheet `Crop details`, mirrored bit-for-bit in `2025_crop_copy.xlsx`). The register documents **1,361 farmer rows** across 26 commercial crop columns.

Deduplication analysis indicates that these 1,361 rows represent approximately **1,335 unique farmers**: AAS group "Durga" in Kailash Nagar was entered twice (rows 692–711 and rows 1348–1367, creating 20 duplicate farmer codes and 26 duplicate rows overall). Row 638 lacks a farmer name.

Out of 1,361 recorded entries, **734 farmers (53.93%)** engaged in active commercial production with positive net income, cultivating a combined physical footprint of **128.645 acres** (125.545 acres excluding zero-income mixed cropping; 128.635 acres among active earners) and generating **₹1,69,78,270.00 (₹1.70 Crore)** in aggregate net income:
- **Mean Net Income across all 1,361 rows**: ₹12,474.85
- **Median Net Income across all 1,361 rows**: ₹3,300.00
- **Mean Net Income among 734 active growers**: ₹23,131.16
- **Median Net Income among active growers**: **₹16,650.00** (prior text claimed ₹17,500)
- **Net Return per Cultivated Acre**: **₹1,31,977.34 per acre** ($\frac{₹1,69,78,270}{128.645 \text{ acres}}$)

**Resolving the Per-Acre Metric Dissonance**:
Earlier synthesis drafts claimed ₹1,36,369.49 was "gross output value" and ₹1,31,973 was "net return". In reality, both figures represent the exact same net income (₹1.70 Crore): ₹1,36,369.49 divides net income by 124.502 acres (an artificial dashboard village subset in `DEHAT_Dash.xlsx`), whereas ₹1,31,977 divides by the true primary register area of 128.645 acres. The register contains zero gross output data.

### 11.3 Agronomic Highlights & Commercial Concentrations
1. **Onion Dominance (43.86% of Revenue)**: Cultivated by 412 farmers generating **₹74.47 Lakhs** across 33.73 recorded acres (true yield: 2,483.5 quintals). Smallholders averaged **₹18,075 per grower** on average plots of 0.082 acres (0.41 local bigha at 0.20 ac/bigha).
2. **Turmeric (*Curcuma longa*)**: 112 farmers generated **₹23.16 Lakhs** on 6.91 physical acres (1,279.0 quintals yield), averaging **₹20,678 per grower** and achieving an exceptional **₹3,35,166 net return per acre**. Turmeric represents the highest value-to-land ratio among major crops (>1 acre), though smaller micro-plots of Ginger (₹50,00,000/ac on 0.078 ac) and Methi (₹8,16,667/ac on 0.09 ac) generated higher nominal quotients.
3. **Lentil Revival**: 135 farmers cultivated 35.10 acres of Masoor dal, generating **₹13.74 Lakhs** (160.40 quintals yield).

### 11.4 The Unmerged Sheet2 Tranche (Empirical Data, Not Speculative Extrapolation)
In `Updated_Fasal Crop wise details_Update _2025.xlsx`, `Sheet2` contains **486 non-empty data rows** (rows 5 to 490), representing **484 named farmers** (470 unique name+father combinations). While lacking column headers, its 117 columns map directly to the layout of `Crop details`.

Earlier synthesis drafts presented speculative extrapolations that Sheet2 represented "~240 active producers generating ₹45–60 Lakh across ~35–45 acres". In reality, Sheet2 contains full empirical crop data:
- **Entity Linkage to Master Roster**: Comparing Sheet2 against `Crop details` reveals that **448 named farmers are completely absent from the master sheet** under exact normalized name+father matching (438 under space normalization, 383 under fuzzy matching).
- **Verified Empirical Totals of the 448 Unmatched Farmers**:
  * **Active Commercial Growers (Income > 0)**: **373 farmers**
  * **Aggregate Net Crop Income**: **₹73,29,076.00 (₹73.29 Lakh)**
  * **Cultivated Land Footprint**: **64.502 acres**
- **Verified Totals for All 486 Sheet2 Rows**: 403 active earners, ₹79,68,576 net income, and 69.741 acres.
- **Combined Programmatic Reach (Crop Details + Sheet2 Unmatched)**:
  $$\text{Unique Farmers} \approx 1,783 \text{ to } 1,809; \quad \text{Aggregate Net Income} = ₹2,43,07,346; \quad \text{Total Footprint} \approx 193.15 \text{ acres}$$
- **Crucial Value Chain Finding**: Sheet2 contains **zero turmeric cultivation** (all 486 rows are blank/zero for turmeric).

## Part 12: Commercial Turmeric Value Chain & Market Intermediation

### 12.1 Programmatic Strategy & Results Framework Realities
Turmeric (*Curcuma longa*) and Ginger (*Zingiber officinale*) formed the centerpiece of the high-value commercialization strategy under Objective 2 of the APPI Results Framework. Results Framework Indicators 9 & 10 set targets of *400 farmers trained on scientific turmeric and ginger cultivation and 120 input supports provided*. 

However, M&E progress reports (HY5/HY6) record that formal departmental input support for turmeric/ginger achieved 0 of the 40 planned deliveries in that cycle. While crop raids by wild boars and spotted deer are documented in field reports (e.g. Bhakuri hamlet), assertions that turmeric was promoted specifically because curcumin is unpalatable to wild herbivores lack documentary citation.

### 12.2 Primary Production Economics vs Grant Report Contradictions
Earlier drafts cited `Grant Report January to December 2025.md` (§5, p. 38), which reported *"137 farmers, ₹46,17,245 revenue, and ₹34,202 average net income"*. 

Forensic auditing reveals that this grant report snapshot was internally contradictory: $\frac{₹46,17,245}{137} = ₹33,702.52$ (the cited average of ₹34,202 implies exactly 135 farmers). More importantly, the primary 2025 production register (`Crop details`) refutes this narrative snapshot:
- **Primary Registered Growers**: Exactly **112 smallholders** (not 137).
- **Physical Cultivated Acreage**: **6.910 physical acres** (Column 105).
- **Total Seed Used (Parsed)**: **6,600.00 kilograms** (Column 106 contains text entries: 94×"50 kg", 13×"100 kg", 2×"150 kg", 1×"200 kg"; earlier claim of 4,120 kg was fabricated).
- **Harvested Yield**: **1,279.00 quintals** (Column 107; earlier claim of 463.20 q was fabricated).
- **Direct Net Revenue**: **₹23,16,000.00 (₹23.16 Lakh)** (Column 108).
- **Mean Net Income per Grower**: **₹20,678.57**.
- **Net Return per Acre**: **₹3,35,166.43 per acre**.
- **Longitudinal FDB Turmeric Adoption**: In the household microdata panel, turmeric adoption expanded from 135 growers (₹7,99,550 net) in 2022 to 216 growers (₹12,97,400) in 2023 and 351 growers (₹29,32,500) in 2024.

### 12.3 Value Chain Linkages & Biographical Grounding
- **Patanjali & Private Herb Linkages**: Grant Report 2024 notes that forward linkages were initiated with Patanjali and the Horticulture Department through Sondi Maati FPO. However, no bulk procurement contracts, formal purchase orders, or institutional sales materialized in the project record.
- **Department of Horticulture Input Support**: In 2025, departmental subsidies totaled ₹1,58,500. However, official itemized records (GR25, pp. 3–4) confirm this funded garlic seed, flower saplings (gladiolus, rose, marigold), and fruit trees (karonda, apple-ber)—**zero turmeric seed was provided**.
- **SFURTI / ODOP Proposals**: Annual reports document exploratory conversations regarding potential leverage of ₹5.0 Crore from SFURTI (Ministry of MSME) and ₹1.0 Crore from ODOP for an FPO-operated processing unit. No formal detailed project reports (DPR) were finalized or approved.
- **Biographical Correction — Rajkumari Devi**: Featured in Grant Report 2025 (pp. 37–38), Rajkumari Devi earned ₹60,000 net profit from turmeric intercropped with *Arbi*. Her actual Gram Panchayat is **Chahalwa** (Kailash Nagar hamlet), not Karikot. Project records document that she cultivates 2 bigha of banana and 1 bigha of turmeric; claims that she leased additional land with profits are unsourced.

## Part 13: Tricolor Dietary Diversity & Seasonal Food Security Matrix

### 13.1 Tricolor Framework & Matrix Geometry
Domain 1 of `DEHAT_Cleaned_Data_with_Acres.csv` operationalizes the **Tricolor Diet** (*तिरंगा भोजन*) monitoring framework across **1,260 columns** covering 36 recall months (January 2022 to December 2024; 12 months × 3 years). Exactly 1,328 out of 1,329 households completed the module (99.9% fill rate).

The 1,260 columns comprise 108 monthly question blocks (3 color categories × 36 months). Each monthly block contains 1 free-text column plus individual binary flags (1 = Consumed, 0 = Not Consumed) for **32 discrete food items**:
- **White Foods (7 items)**: Milk, Cheese/Paneer, Egg, Cauliflower, Rice, Option 6, Other White.
- **Green Foods (15 items)**: Bitter gourd, Bottle gourd, Cucumber, Jackfruit, Lemon, Pointed gourd, Pumpkin, Banana, Cowpea, Drumstick leaf (*Sahjan*), Green leafy vegetables (*Saag*), Kundru, Ladyfinger, Ridge gourd, Other Green.
- **Yellow Foods (10 items)**: Chickpea, Lentil/Masoor, Maize, Pigeon Pea/Arhar, Soybean, Banana, Muskmelon, Peas Dal, Wheat, Other Yellow.

*Crucial Data Boundary*: Narrative claims that the microdata captures "self-grown from nutrition gardens versus market-purchased for each item" are incorrect. While this attribute appeared on the physical paper booklet, it was **never digitized into KoboToolbox or the CSV**.

### 13.2 Longitudinal Pulse Intake Expansion (+11.27% vs +12.61%)
Earlier synthesis drafts asserted that pulse consumption expanded by "+11.27%", citing the APPI Results Framework and claiming that an 8.61 percentage-point gain (from 68.25% to 76.86% = 12.61% relative growth) "directly matched" 11.27%.

Forensic investigation reveals the true origin of this figure:
- The figure **+11.27%** originates from the external Midline Evaluation report (`Midline_FASAL_Jaykar (1).docx`), which evaluated a **5-item legume composite** that combined the 4 Yellow-list pulses with **Cowpea (*Lobhiya*) from the Green list**.
- Summing mean monthly consuming households across these 5 items yields:
  $$\text{2022: } 4,299.67 \quad \longrightarrow \quad \text{2023: } 4,408.83 \quad \longrightarrow \quad \text{2024: } 4,784.17 \text{ HH-months}$$
  $$\text{Relative Growth} = \frac{4,784.17 - 4,299.67}{4,299.67} = \mathbf{+11.27\%}$$
- When restricting analysis strictly to the 4 Yellow pulses (Pigeon pea, Lentil, Peas dal, Chickpea), the mean monthly consuming households expand from 3,628.17 to 4,085.75, representing **+12.61% relative growth (+8.61 percentage points)**.
- The APPI Results Framework template contains no pulse indicator; the indicator belongs to the Midline evaluation.

```
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| VERIFIED LONGITUDINAL LEGUME CONSUMPTION TRAJECTORY (1,329 HOUSEHOLDS × 36 RECALL MONTHS)                                         |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Legume Variety / Category             | 2022 Baseline Prevalence | 2023 Midline Prevalence | 2024 Endline Prevalence | 2-Year Net Change  |
+---------------------------------------+--------------------------+-------------------------+-------------------------+--------------------+
| Pigeon Pea / Arhar (*Cajanus cajan*)  | 91.06% of HH-Months      | 91.72% of HH-Months     | 91.96% of HH-Months     | +0.90 pp (+0.99%)  |
| Lentils / Masoor (*Lens culinaris*)   | 81.72% of HH-Months      | 82.96% of HH-Months     | 93.99% of HH-Months     | +12.27 pp (+15.01%)|
| Green Peas Dal (*Pisum sativum*)      | 56.91% of HH-Months      | 60.03% of HH-Months     | 62.56% of HH-Months     | +5.65 pp (+9.93%)  |
| Chickpea / Chana (*Cicer arietinum*)  | 43.32% of HH-Months      | 45.74% of HH-Months     | 58.92% of HH-Months     | +15.60 pp (+36.01%)|
+---------------------------------------+--------------------------+-------------------------+-------------------------+--------------------+
| **4-Pulse Composite (Yellow List)**   | **68.25% Average**       | **70.11% Average**      | **76.86% Average**      | **+8.61 pp (+12.6%)|
| **5-Item Midline Composite (+Cowpea)**| **4,299.7 HH-Months**    | **4,408.8 HH-Months**   | **4,784.2 HH-Months**   | **+11.27% Relative**|
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

### 13.3 Nutritional Seasonality: Inverting the "Hunger Gap" Myth
Earlier drafts claimed that green food consumption collapsed to 31.4% in June/July 2022 ("summer hunger gap") and was subsequently "eliminated by 1,328 nutrition gardens".

Microdata recomputation completely refutes this claim:
- **Summer is the Annual Peak**: In reality, summer is the **peak of green vegetable intake**, driven by pumpkin, cucumber, bitter gourd, and bottle gourd. The mean share of the 14 named green items consumed was **67.2% in June 2022, 68.8% in June 2023, and 69.4% in June 2024**.
- **Winter is the Seasonal Trough**: The annual low occurs in **January (33.0%–34.1%) and February (34.1%–35.4%)**, when summer vines have died and winter vegetables are limited.
- **Universal Green Intake**: Over **99.5% of households consumed at least one green item in every single calendar month** across all three years.
- **Nutrition Garden Clarification**: The survey contains zero variables on nutrition gardens; "1,328" was simply the total count of households reporting food data.

### 14.2 Forensic Dissection of the Prior ₹11.70 Crore Claim
Earlier synthesis drafts claimed **₹11,70,21,520 across 583 projects**. This headline figure was completely fabricated by summing spreadsheet total and subtotal rows:
1. **The 2025 Doubling Error**: In `1. Leverage AASs 2025.xlsx`, rows 7–164 contain 158 genuine items totaling ₹2,78,24,960. Row 165 is the total row (`=SUM(G7:G164)`). The prior script summed the entire column including row 165:
   $$₹2,78,24,960 + ₹2,78,24,960 = \mathbf{₹5,56,49,920} \quad (\text{exactly } 2.0\times)$$
2. **The 2023 Subtotal Distortion**: In `Levrage January to December 2023.xlsx`, the 259 items in Column G sum to ₹2,35,45,200. Row 264 is a subtotal row (`=SUBTOTAL(9,G103:G263)`) evaluating to ₹1,48,33,200. The prior script summed Column G including row 264 ($₹2,35,45,200 + ₹1,48,33,200 = \mathbf{₹3,83,78,400}$), while missing 11 road projects (rows 66–73, 100–102) whose sanctioned amount sat exclusively in Column H (₹27,35,000).
3. **The 583 Item Count**: The count of 583 claims arose from summing data rows alongside footer rows: 260 in 2023 (259 items + 1 subtotal row), 163 in 2024 (160 items + 3 footer rows: Hindi text, date, signatures), and 160 in 2025 (158 items + 2 footer rows). Genuine project items total **577**.

**Key Contrast with Prior Narrative Claims**:
1. **Roads & Drainage**: Prior text claimed ₹7,72,50,000 (66.01% of total). True multi-year expenditure across 50 roads and 10 drains is **₹2,86,01,200 (37.10%)**. In 2025, roads total **₹58,00,000** (3 RCC ₹21.5 L, 1 pakki ₹30 L, 2 kachcha repairs ₹6.5 L), NOT ₹4.40 Crore.
2. **Har Ghar Nal (Piped Water)**: Prior text claimed ₹22,50,000 across 225 taps. True register value is **₹94,50,000 across 665 tap connections** (Rajaram Tanda: 225 taps / ₹22.50 L; Jamuniha: 300 taps / ₹30.0 L; Narayan Tanda: 80 taps / ₹24.0 L; Vishun Tanda: 60 taps / ₹18.0 L).
3. **PMAY Repeat Beneficiaries**: While 280 lines exist across registers, fuzzy entity matching identifies **~20 repeat beneficiaries across consecutive years** (17 between 2023 and 2024; 3 between 2024 and 2025; one beneficiary, Gudiya w/o Jitendra, appears in all three registers: 2023 r13, 2024 r13, 2025 r17). Thus, there are approximately **260 unique recipient households**.
4. **Register Semantics**: None of the registers record actual disbursement dates, sanction numbers, or completion receipts. Amounts reflect official scheme unit norms (PMAY = ₹1,20,000; toilet = ₹12,000; soak pit = ₹11,000–25,000). Terms like "audited" or "verified delivery" must be replaced with "community-mobilized scheme proposals and entitlement applications valued at official unit norms".

### 14.4 True Register Semantics & Resolving the Dashboard "Gap"
Earlier synthesis drafts claimed that the figure of **₹8,25,13,000** reported in `DEHAT_Dash.xlsx` (Rights & Entitlements Dashboard) was a "conservative subset" that excluded a "₹4.40 Crore 2025 road project."

This reconciliation narrative was entirely fictitious:
- In `DEHAT_Dash.xlsx`, the dashboard total of **₹8,25,13,360** is simply:
  $$\begin{aligned}
  \text{2022 (FASAL HY2 Progress Report):} &\quad ₹54,14,000.00 \\
  \text{2023 (Primary Workbook, Cell H264):} &\quad ₹2,62,81,200.00 \\
  \text{2024 (Primary Workbook Total):} &\quad ₹2,29,93,200.00 \\
  \text{2025 (Primary Workbook Total):} &\quad ₹2,78,24,960.00 \\
  \hline
  \mathbf{\text{Dashboard Grand Total:}} &\quad \mathbf{₹8,25,13,360.00} \quad [\approx \mathbf{₹8.25 \text{ Crore}}]
  \end{aligned}$$
- The dashboard was completely accurate all along. The supposed "gap" existed only because the synthesis created an artificial surplus through its own double-counting defects. No ₹4.40 Crore road project ever existed.

**Forensic Deconstruction of JSK Claims**:
1. **The "151 PM-Kisan Registrations" Myth (B08)**: The prior synthesis claimed all 151 rows of the `2025` sheet were "PM-Kisan registrations/renewals". In reality, the sheet is an uncurated field log containing **four distinct sub-lists**:
   - *Rows 2–23*: **PM-Kisan Registrations**: Exactly **12 actual farmer entries** carrying registration IDs (`UP...`), followed by 10 completely blank serial placeholders (rows 14–23).
   - *Rows 25–57*: **Horticulture Dept Seed Booking**: 32 rows booking onion and garlic seed (*"उद्यान विभाग एकीकृत बागवानी विकास मिसन... प्याज लहसुन बीज बुकिंग"*).
   - *Rows 62–139*: **Natural Farming Drum Distribution**: 77 rows receiving 200L plastic drums for Jeevamrit (75 in Fakirpuri) (*"नेशनल मिसन फॉर नेचुरल फार्मिंग... 200 लीटर ड्रम वितरण"*).
   - *Rows 144–158*: **Cucurbit Seed Booking**: 14 rows booking bitter gourd and bottle gourd seed.
   - Total genuine records in the `2025` sheet: **135 records** (plus 3 titles, 3 headers, and 10 blank serials = 151 rows).
2. **Borewell Tube-Well Applications (B07)**: The 352 rows in `boring avedan 2024` are applications (*avedan*). No sanction dates, installation verifications, or operational statuses are recorded; 4 registration numbers are duplicates. Assertions that these applications "ended diesel dependence" are ungrounded.
3. **Absence of Outcome / Resolution Tracking**: None of the four sheets track application approvals, rejections, or completion status. All claims regarding an "80% resolution rate", "conversion funnels", or "application-to-sanction efficiency" are ungrounded.

### 15.2 FASAL Capacity Building & Training MIS (210 Sessions, 13,510 Attendances)
Primary file `FASAL Training data.xlsx` (sheet `Training `) catalogs **210 actual conducted training and stakeholder interface sessions** between May 3, 2022 and December 6, 2026, mobilizing a cumulative attendance of **13,510 participant attendances**:
- **Session Count**: Rows 2 to 215 contain serial numbers 1 to 214. However, rows 212 to 215 (SL 211 to 214) are completely blank placeholders containing only a serial number. There are exactly **210 documented sessions** (NOT 214).
- **Attendances vs Participants**: The sum of the `Total Participants` column across all 210 sessions is **13,510 attendances**. Earlier drafts misreported this as "12,510" due to an addition error (the synthesis's own 14 table rows summed to 13,510). Crucially, these represent **cumulative event attendances**, NOT unique individuals trained (e.g. one village interface meeting on row 87 had 1,342 attendees).
- **Curricula Classification**: Exactly 14 distinct non-empty titles across three operational tiers:

**Data Hygiene Notes**:
- Six sessions have dates recorded after the file's last modified date (7 August 2026), including sessions dated 6 November and 6 December 2026, representing date-entry typos by field staff.
- Two exact duplicate session entries exist (same date, title, and count: 15-Oct-2022 ×57; 28-Aug-2023 ×42).

### 15.3 AAS Monthly Meetings & Review Presentation Realities
- **Monthly Presentation Inventory**: The workspace contains 28 presentation files (`.pptx`) across 2024 and 2025 folders. SHA-256 byte hashing confirms **6 byte-identical duplicate pairs**, leaving exactly **22 unique review presentations** reviewing 22 distinct operational months.
- **Meeting Compliance**: Monthly targets were 74 to 80 meetings. While earlier drafts claimed an achievement of "50 to 65 meetings (70–85% compliance)", primary review slides reveal that achieved meetings ranged widely from **12 to 68 meetings (16% to 92%)**. During the monsoon season (July to September), meeting frequency dropped sharply to **12 to 38 meetings** (16%–51% compliance) due to flooding and peak agricultural operations.
- **CM Helpline (1076)**: Appears in only 4 monthly decks, recording complaint registration counts. **Zero resolution data exists**. The claim of an "over 80% resolution rate" was fabricated.
